13 
II. 
Purpose of Testimony 
14 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Panel’s testimony? 
15 
A. 
As the energy provider to nearly two million customers in downstate 
16 
New York, the Companies are committed to advancing clean energy 
17 
solutions that further National Grid’s commitment to environmental 
18 
stewardship.  Today, demand for natural gas remains strong, as 
19 
customers seek a cost-effective, reliable heating source that generates 
20 
fewer emissions than alternatives such as heavy oil.  In this way, natural 
21 


--- Page 10 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
7 of 120 
 
gas continues to play a critical role in driving economic opportunity in 
1 
New York.  Yet, with the challenges presented by climate change, the 
2 
State, the Commission, and the Companies recognize that more is needed 
3 
to meaningfully change the current climate trajectory.   
4 
 
5 
Governor Cuomo committed to aggressively pursuing clean energy 
6 
policies aimed at reducing greenhouse gas emissions from the energy 
7 
sector 40 percent (from 1990 levels) by 2030 and the longer-term goal of 
8 
decreasing total carbon emissions 80 percent by 2050, as well as an 
9 
aggressive new renewable energy goal that 100 percent of electricity 
10 
consumed in New York be carbon neutral by 2040.  For its part, National 
11 
Grid similarly launched its “Northeast 80x50 Pathway” (the “80x50 
12 
Pathway”) complementing New York State’s efforts.  The Companies’ 
13 
gas networks will play an integral role in meeting these ambitious goals 
14 
and delivering the low-carbon economy of the future.   
15 
 
16 
The purpose of this testimony is to set forth the Companies’ vision for 
17 
the future of the heating sector; a vision aimed at achieving those shared 
18 
goals through a comprehensive plan built on the Companies’ core 
19 
obligations to provide safe, reliable, and affordable gas service to 
20 
customers in New York.  As more fully discussed below, the Companies 
21 


--- Page 11 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
8 of 120 
 
are sponsoring a suite of proposals directed at: (i) reducing methane 
1 
emissions from the gas distribution system; (ii) achieving greater energy 
2 
efficiency; (iii) promoting demand response and other non-pipes 
3 
alternatives (“NPAs”); (iv) encouraging the development of sustainable 
4 
heating options; (v) promoting economic development through 
5 
investment in energy infrastructure; (vi) reducing carbon emissions on 
6 
the gas network; and (vii) supporting further research, development, and 
7 
demonstration (“RD&D”) to advance RNG technologies.   
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ vision for the future of the heating 
10 
sector. 
11 
A. 
National Grid’s vision for a sustainable gas future in New York is 
12 
founded on a core commitment to exceed the expectations of its 
13 
customers and communities while making possible the energy systems of 
14 
tomorrow.  Delivering on this vision requires a proactive, customer-
15 
centric, mission-driven approach – an approach that combines the 
16 
Companies’ ongoing obligation to provide safe, affordable, and reliable 
17 
gas service with a renewed focus on advancing clean energy goals.  To 
18 
do this, the Companies developed a four-pronged strategy that establishes 
19 
the goals, tools, and incentives for driving meaningful change in the 
20 
heating sector: 
21 


--- Page 12 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
9 of 120 
 
Reducing methane emissions from the gas distribution system 60 percent 
1 
by 2035: Building on the 80x50 Pathway, National Grid proposes an 
2 
aggressive goal of reducing total network methane emissions 60 percent 
3 
by 2035; continuing its leadership role in national initiatives aimed at 
4 
reducing emissions; identifying, prioritizing, and repairing large-system 
5 
leaks; and implementing work procedures to further reduce emissions 
6 
going forward.  
7 
 
8 
Empowering and Enabling Customers to Sustainably Meet Their Heating 
9 
Needs: The Companies have developed a suite of programs, products, 
10 
and demonstration projects aimed at empowering and enabling customers 
11 
to take control of their energy usage through: incorporation of 
12 
accelerated energy efficiency goals consistent with budgets and targets 
13 
set forth in the Commission’s December 2018 EE Order; access to RNG 
14 
supplies through a Green Gas Tariff offering; an expanded Gas Demand 
15 
Response Demonstration Project that complements the Companies’ 
16 
robust interruptible (“IT”) and temperature control (“TC”) service 
17 
offerings; the development of a Sustainable Heat Initiative that includes 
18 
deployment of geothermal heat pump technology, support for clean oil-
19 
to-gas conversions, and additional heating-related technology for low-to-
20 
moderate income (“LMI”) customers; as well as enhanced economic 
21 


--- Page 13 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
10 of 120 
 
development programs capable of driving environmentally beneficial 
1 
outcomes.  
2 
 
3 
Reducing Carbon Emissions on the Gas Network:  The integration of 
4 
RNG and other sustainable gas supplies will reduce the carbon footprint 
5 
of the Companies’ gas networks.  To facilitate carbon reductions through 
6 
the integration of RNG supplies, the Companies propose to: deliver the 
7 
Newtown Creek RNG project – one of the first projects in the U.S. to 
8 
directly inject RNG into a local distribution system using biogas 
9 
generated from a wastewater treatment plant and food waste; coordinate 
10 
with the Department of Energy’s National Renewable Energy Laboratory 
11 
(“NREL”) and potentially partner with New York City (“NYC”) to 
12 
develop a power-to-gas (“P2G”) demonstration project that will convert 
13 
excess renewable electricity to RNG and show how the gas system can 
14 
serve as a long-term storage solution for renewable energy supplies; 
15 
study, in coordination with Stony Brook University’s Institute of Gas 
16 
Innovation and Technology (“I-GIT”), the opportunities and challenges 
17 
with blending hydrogen into the gas distribution system; eliminate 
18 
barriers to RNG interconnections, and facilitate and incentivize new 
19 
RNG projects through Company ownership of portions of the 
20 


--- Page 14 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
11 of 120 
 
interconnection equipment; and continuing productive end-use RD&D 
1 
activities. 
2 
 
3 
Performance-Based Incentives and Revenue Sharing: To align the 
4 
Companies’ incentives with a sustainable vision for the future of the 
5 
heating sector and overarching energy policy goals, the Companies 
6 
propose three Earnings Adjustment Mechanisms (“EAMs”), two 
7 
Platform Service Revenue (“PSR”) opportunities, and a NPA Incentive 
8 
Mechanism. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the anticipated benefits of the Companies’ Future of 
11 
Heat strategy. 
12 
A. 
The Companies’ multi-faceted approach empowers customers to make 
13 
energy choices that further clean energy goals, while also establishing the 
14 
Companies as key drivers of the energy transition through development 
15 
and deployment of RNG and a suite of NPA technologies.  Collectively, 
16 
the Companies believe the solutions presented here will drive a positive 
17 
change in how customers meet their energy requirements and have a 
18 
beneficial impact on the environment with projected reductions in carbon 
19 
dioxide (“CO2”) emissions of approximately 73,000 metric tons 
20 
(equivalent to taking 15,000 cars off the road for one year). 
21 


--- Page 15 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel

6 
III. 
Reducing Methane Emissions 
7 
Q. 
What is methane and why are the Companies focused on reducing 
8 
emissions? 
9 
A. 
Methane (CH4), the primary component of natural gas, has been 
10 
identified by the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (“EPA”) as a 
11 
greenhouse gas.  The Companies, as distributors of natural gas, are 
12 
committed to taking actions that reduce methane emissions from the gas 
13 
network, ensuring that gas deliveries are sustainable and consistent with 
14 
the Companies’ clean energy goals.   
15 
 
16 
Q. 
What steps have the Companies taken to address methane 
17 
emissions? 
18 
A. 
The most cost-effective means of reducing methane emissions is to 
19 
eliminate system leaks through the replacement of aging infrastructure.  
20 
As shown in Figure 1 below, between 2014 and 2018, the Companies 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
15 of 120 
 
replaced approximately 733 miles of leak-prone pipe (“LPP”), preventing 
1 
approximately 3,388 metric tons of methane emissions each year.1     
2 
Figure 1: Methane Emission Reductions 
3 
 17,000
 18,000
 19,000
 20,000
 21,000
 22,000
 23,000
2014
2015
2016
2017
2018
mt CH4
GHGRP Reporting Year
Methane Emissions
 
4 
In addition to infrastructure upgrades, the Companies have implemented 
5 
work practices and procedures to mitigate methane emissions released 
6 
during construction and maintenance activities.  As discussed more 
7 
below, in May 2016, the Companies established a blowdown protocol 
8 
that limits methane emissions when purging gas from a pipeline – which 
9 
can otherwise significantly contribute to total system emissions. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
How do the Companies plan to build on these efforts? 
12 
                                                 
1 Emission reduction estimates are based on the calculation methodology set forth in the 
Environmental Protection Agency’s (“EPA”) regulations (40 C.F.R. § 98, Subpart W). 


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
16 of 120 
 
A. 
The Companies are building on their initial success by deploying a multi-
1 
faceted strategy aimed at achieving meaningful methane reductions, 
2 
including a proposal to increase the pace and scale of LPP replacement in 
3 
NYC, identifying and repairing the highest emitting leaks, deploying a 
4 
new mobile methane detection program, and continued utilization of 
5 
blowdown reduction procedures. 
6 
 
7 
 
A. 
Commitment to Methane Reduction Policies 
8 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ efforts to lead the policy discussion 
9 
on reducing methane emissions.    
10 
A. 
To drive a positive step change in natural gas production and distribution, 
11 
the Companies have taken a series of policy actions aimed at reducing 
12 
methane emissions.  First, the Companies are establishing an ambitious 
13 
target to reduce methane emissions from the gas distribution system 60 
14 
percent (from 1990 levels) by 2035.  Second, as part of the EPA’s 
15 
Methane Challenge Program’s ONE Future Emissions Intensity 
16 
Commitment Option, the Companies are working to reduce the methane 
17 
leakage rate from the production, processing, transmission, and 
18 
distribution of natural gas to one percent or less by 2025.  Collectively, 
19 
these policy initiatives are driving transparency and accountability for 
20 


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
17 of 120 
 
reducing methane emissions across all segments of the natural gas value 
1 
chain.  
2 
 
3 
B. 
Leak-Prone Pipe Replacement and Leak Reduction Programs 
4 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ LPP replacement efforts. 
5 
A. 
The direct testimony of KEDNY and KEDLI’s Gas Infrastructure and 
6 
Operations Panels (“GIOP”) highlight the Companies’ continuing 
7 
commitment to replacing older, high-risk LPP, which disproportionately 
8 
contributes to leaks on the Companies’ systems.  The EPA, as part of its 
9 
Methane Challenge Program, identified this strategy – upgrading natural 
10 
gas infrastructure – as a best management practice for reducing methane 
11 
emissions; noting that it also mitigates operational risks, increases 
12 
efficiency, and advances environmental goals.   
13 
 
14 
To that end, the Companies propose to continue their aggressive 
15 
proactive LPP replacement efforts, increasing the replacement miles to 
16 
225 per year for a total of 930 miles over four years.  Altogether, the 
17 
Companies anticipate these efforts, once complete, will result in 
18 
greenhouse gas reductions of approximately 3,627 metric tons of 
19 
methane each year. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 21 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
18 of 120 
 
Q. 
What are the Companies’ commitments to reducing system leaks? 
1 
A. 
Over the last several years, the Companies have eliminated thousands of 
2 
leaks from their backlogs of non-hazardous leaks, which has driven 
3 
further reductions in overall methane emissions.  Each year, KEDNY and 
4 
KEDLI eliminate more than 150 and 750 system leaks, respectively, 
5 
through a combination of main/service replacements and proactive leak 
6 
repairs.  The Companies are additionally incentivized to repair up to 250 
7 
incremental Type 3 leaks that have been ranked as high emitters - and 
8 
these incentive targets were achieved in 2017 and 2018.  In this case, the 
9 
Companies are proposing to maintain aggressive leak reductions targets 
10 
that would reduce the Companies’ total system leaks by at least 900 leaks 
11 
each year, as well as retain the current incentive to repair the highest 
12 
emitting system leaks.  These leak reduction targets are discussed in 
13 
more detail by the Companies’ Gas Safety Panel. 
14 
 
15 
 
C. 
Mobile Methane Detection Program 
16 
Q. 
What is mobile methane detection? 
17 
A. 
Mobile methane detection uses sensors (e.g., cavity ring-down 
18 
spectroscopy) attached to a vehicle equipped with mapping technology.  
19 
This technology has the potential to quickly and efficiently identify 
20 
large-volume natural gas leaks.  With this information, utilities can target 
21 


--- Page 22 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
19 of 120 
 
infrastructure repairs and upgrades in high-priority areas with significant 
1 
leaks.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ mobile methane detection efforts. 
4 
A. 
The Companies, in partnership with Google, the Environmental Defense 
5 
Fund, and Colorado State University, have been evaluating the potential 
6 
use of an enhanced high-emitter methane detection vehicle, also referred 
7 
to as the “Google Vehicle,” to help with leak identification and 
8 
prioritization.  As set forth in the direct testimony of KEDNY and 
9 
KEDLI’s Gas Safety Panel, the Companies are proposing a program to 
10 
expand the use of this technology in high-leak concentration areas.  The 
11 
technology could prove useful in monitoring, mapping, and helping the 
12 
Companies to quickly and cost-effectively evaluate leak size and volume.  
13 
With that information, the Companies can, in turn, prioritize repair and 
14 
replacement activities in areas that can achieve the greatest methane 
15 
emission reductions.  Importantly, this effort is focused on identifying 
16 
environmentally significant leaks, and as such it will augment, not 
17 
replace, the Companies traditional leak surveying methods of identifying 
18 
leaks.   
19 
 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 23 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
20 of 120 
 
 
D. 
Blowdown Procedures 
1 
Q. 
What are blowdown procedures and how do they help reduce 
2 
methane emissions? 
3 
A. 
Natural gas distributors use blowdown mitigation procedures to bleed 
4 
down and evacuate natural gas from an isolated pipeline or vessel and 
5 
then re-inject the gas into the distribution system instead of letting it 
6 
escape into the atmosphere.  With the isolated pipeline free of gas, the 
7 
pipeline owner or operator can then safely commence construction and 
8 
repair activities.  The EPA’s Methane Challenge Program, through its 
9 
Natural Gas STAR Program, identified the injection of blowdown gas 
10 
into low-pressure mains as a best management practice for reducing 
11 
methane emissions. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ blowdown procedures. 
14 
A. 
In May 2016, National Grid adopted procedures for “Drawdown 
15 
Compressor to Bleed Down Evacuate and Re-Inject Natural Gas from an 
16 
Isolated Pipeline.”  The Companies use the procedures to minimize 
17 
methane emissions when performing work in furtherance of their 
18 
Methane Challenge Program commitments and clean energy priorities.  
19 
In 2018, the Companies estimate the blowdown procedures reduced 
20 
methane emissions by 156.442 million cubic feet (mcf).   
21 


--- Page 24 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
21 of 120

Customer Empowerment and Enablement 
1

2 
of heat?

4

5 
future benefits, while continuing to deliver safe, affordable, and reliable 
6 
service.  In practice, this means seamlessly enhancing the customer 
7

8 
through robust energy efficiency offerings; advancing new products and 
9 
services that allow customers to actively participate in achieving clean 
10 
energy goals; and enabling new customers to sustainably meet their 
11 
heating needs.

1. 
Green Gas Tariff Offering 
5 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ proposed Green Gas Tariff offering. 
6 
A. 
As set forth in Exhibit ___ (FOH-1), Schedule 1, the Companies propose 
7 
a Green Gas Tariff offering beginning in Data Year 1 that will enable 
8 
customers to voluntarily purchase RNG to meet all or a portion of their 
9 
energy needs.  The offering will include four tiers, allowing customers to 
10 
select a level of green gas procurement that works for their budget and 
11 
their environmental aspirations.   
12

15

16 
while non-residential customers tend to prefer costs that scale as a 
17 
percentage of use.  To accommodate these differing customer 
18 
preferences, the Companies propose a tiered structure as follows

Q. 
What are the benefits associated with the proposed Green Gas Tariff 
16 
offering?  
17 
A. 
Offering a Green Gas Tariff to customers means they will have the 
18 
opportunity to purchase a renewable fuel without the need to modify 
19 
their existing equipment.  Because RNG is made up of the same 
20 
constituents as fossil-based natural gas, it can be introduced into the gas 
21 


--- Page 37 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
34 of 120 
 
distribution network safely and utilized by existing natural gas-fired 
1 
equipment.  This allows customers to reduce the environmental impact of 
2 
their heating, without the need to replace appliances.  Adoption by 
3 
customers will also help advance the RNG market by providing 
4 
predictable demand and certainty to RNG developers looking to invest in 
5 
RNG projects.  Additionally, customers who operate NGVs can purchase 
6 
RNG for their fleet, helping decarbonize transportation (the sector with 
7 
the largest volume of carbon emissions in the State).  Finally, the Green 
8 
Gas Tariff directly supports clean energy policy objectives, including: 
9 
achieving a 40 percent reduction in greenhouse gas emissions by 2030; 
10 
National Grid’s 80x50 Pathway; cleaner transportation; protecting New 
11 
York’s natural resources by diverting waste streams and reducing 
12 
organics in the water system; and building a more resilient energy 
13 
system.

2. 
Utility Energy Services Contracts (“UESC”) Program 
16 
Q. 
What is the UESC program? 
17 
A. 
The UESC program is an energy services contracting program, where the 
18 
Companies and customers, typically government agencies and other large 
19 
commercial 
entities, 
enter 
limited-source 
contracts 
for 
energy 
20 
management services, such as energy and water improvements.  With a 
21 


--- Page 38 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
35 of 120 
 
UESC, the Companies can help customers analyze their current energy 
1 
usage, identify opportunities for improvement, and work with pre-
2 
selected contractors to achieve efficiencies, reduce costs, and adopt 
3 
renewable energy systems, provided the efficiencies include some 
4 
measure of gas savings.  The customers, in turn, pay for the contracted 
5 
services for the improvements delivered under the UESC.  As further set 
6 
forth in Exhibit ___ (FOH-1), Schedule 2, the Companies and their New 
7 
York affiliates have participated in UESCs since 2011, delivering more 
8 
than $18 million in value.    
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Are the Companies proposing any changes to the UESC program? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies propose an incentive to help customers offset the 
12 
cost of energy feasibility studies, as well as the inclusion of a marketing 
13 
budget to engage new customers.  The Companies also propose a PSR 
14 
for fees received from entities who enter a UESC with either of the 
15 
Companies to implement the feasibility study findings.  The PSR 
16 
proposal is set forth in the Performance-Based Incentives and Revenue 
17 
Sharing Section below. 
18 
19 


--- Page 39 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
36 of 120 
 
Q. 
Please describe the proposed energy feasibility study incentive. 
1 
A. 
Feasibility studies are often the first step in the UESC process, providing 
2 
customers with a range of improvements that could help better manage 
3 
their energy usage and the potential cost savings associated with 
4 
implementing the study’s findings.  The studies, however, can be 
5 
expensive, with upfront costs deterring customers from initiating the 
6 
UESC process.  To overcome this hurdle, and only in so far as such costs 
7 
are not covered by another program or entity like NYSERSDA, the 
8 
Companies propose an incentive equivalent to 50 percent of any 
9 
feasibility study that exceeds $0.010 million.   
10

15 
customer leads), sales, and procurement.  The FTEs will include a 
16 
marketing manager to drive additional customer participation in the 
17 
program and a project engineer to assist customers through the energy 
18 
feasibility study and implementation process.

C. 
Demand Response 
4 
Q. 
What role does demand response play in the Companies’ future of 
5 
heat strategy? 
6 
A. 
Demand response has the potential to play an important role in the 
7 
Companies’ future of heat strategy.  Accordingly, the Companies are 
8 
promoting demand response in two ways.  First, the Companies’ IT/TC 
9 
service is one of the largest gas demand response programs in the 
10 
country.  Among other things, IT/TC service enables the Companies to 
11 
reduce gas demand during periods of peak usage by offering a reduced 
12 
delivery rate to customers who agree to interrupt service when demand 
13 
peaks.  The program benefits all customers by allowing the Companies to 
14 
utilize the gas system more efficiently while avoiding the need for costly 
15 
capital upgrades to meet a higher peak demand.  Following a 
16 
collaborative with Staff and interested parties, the Companies proposed 
17 
to implement certain enhancements to the IT/TC offering.  In the 
18 
February 7, 2019 Order Approving Tariff Revisions and Directing 
19 
Further Tariff Filings in Cases 16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059 (“IT/TC 
20 
Order”), the Commission adopted these changes, which will blend the 
21 


--- Page 45 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
42 of 120 
 
existing IT and TC services into a single non-firm service classification 
1 
with two pricing tiers determined by a customer’s fuel switching 
2 
capabilities.  The testimony of KEDNY and KEDLI’s Rate Design 
3 
Panels discuss the proposed enhancements to IT/TC services.   
4 
 
5 
Second, to complement the IT/TC program, the Companies’ propose to 
6 
expand the existing Demand Response Demonstration Project for firm 
7 
customers who commit to minimum reductions.  Expanding the project 
8 
will allow the Companies to better determine whether they can achieve 
9 
system benefits through peak demand reductions.  Taken together, the 
10 
non-firm and firm demand response programs have potential as 
11 
components of the Companies’ suite of NPA planning tools.   
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Please describe the Gas Demand Response Program adopted by the 
14 
Commission in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases. 
15 
A. 
Under the Gas Demand Response Program, the Companies provide a 
16 
market-based credit to commercial firm customers who reduce their gas 
17 
usage by a pre-determined amount when called upon by either company 
18 
to do so.  The initial purpose of the Demand Response Demonstration 
19 
Project was to assess the effectiveness of voluntary peak reductions in 
20 


--- Page 46 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
43 of 120 
 
terms of reducing intraday demand and whether market-based credits 
1 
will drive customer behavior to reduce consumption. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What results have the Companies seen thus far? 
4 
A. 
In the project’s first year, the Companies collectively called five events 
5 
(two were exclusive to KEDLI customers), with 100 percent of the 16 
6 
project enrollees participating.  During events in which both KEDNY 
7 
and KEDLI customers participated, they collectively contributed a 
8 
reduction of 192 dekatherm (“Dth”) per hour of nameplate capacity (135 
9 
Dth in KEDNY, 57 Dth in KEDLI) during the peak hour.  Such a 
10 
reduction alleviates the burden on the system, and, with sufficient 
11 
assurances, could be considered a system-management tool in the future.  
12 
As part of the demonstration, the Companies provided $0.303 million in 
13 
incentive payments to customers.   
14 
 
15 
For the current year, only one customer has withdrawn from the project 
16 
for operational reasons and a large property management company, 
17 
Estates NY Real Estate Services LLC (“Estates”), has shown 
18 
considerable interest in participating, submitting multiple applications for 
19 
different facilities.  The Companies were able to incorporate one of the 
20 
facilities into the project – creating further opportunities for collaboration 
21 


--- Page 47 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
44 of 120 
 
and learning as discussed by the Commission in the IT/TC Order.  
1 
During the second year, the Companies collectively called five events 
2 
(two were exclusive to KEDLI customers).  Customer participation 
3 
across all events was 94 percent.  In the events with 100 percent 
4 
participation, customers provided 241 Dth per hour of nameplate 
5 
capacity (184 Dth in KEDNY and 57 Dth in KEDLI) during the peak 
6 
hour.  Further information on the Demand Response Demonstration 
7 
Project is included in Exhibit ___(FOH-3). 
8 
 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ proposed expansion of the Demand 
11 
Response Demonstration Project. 
12 
A. 
The Companies propose to build off the initial success of the Demand 
13 
Response Demonstration Project, expanding it in scale and continuing to 
14 
market to firm gas delivery customers that meet minimum annual 
15 
demand values and agree to a minimum reduction amount (Dth/event 
16 
hour).  The Companies will notify enrolled customers in advance of an 
17 
event.  Such customers will then receive an incentive for reducing their 
18 
gas demand by the pre-determined, minimum reduction value for the 
19 
entire duration of a peak event.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 48 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
45 of 120 
 
Reductions in excess of the committed minimum will be incentivized at a 
1 
prorated amount.  The reduction will be determined based on a baseline 
2 
that will be developed for each participating customer.  To achieve the 
3 
level of certainty needed for firm customer demand response to provide 
4 
meaningful system benefits, the Companies propose to include a penalty 
5 
that exceeds the incentive for customers who fail to respond or fail to 
6 
meet the minimum committed reduction.  Furthermore, customers who 
7 
fail to respond or meet their committed reduction for more than a 
8 
predetermined number of events may be removed from the project and 
9 
prohibited from participating in future years.  Further detail regarding the 
10 
proposed expansion of the Demand Response Demonstration Project is 
11 
included in Exhibit ___ (FOH-1), Schedule 5. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What are the benefits associated with the expanded Gas Demand 
14 
Response Project?  
15 
A. 
Gas demand response is a potentially valuable tool in the Companies’ 
16 
NPA toolbox.  The project has already illustrated the capability to 
17 
meaningfully decrease system pressure during peak periods.  Customers 
18 
have also proven willing to reduce their gas usage in response to 
19 
financial incentives.  Structural changes to the program, including the use 
20 
of a baseline to determine reductions and increasing the scale of the 
21 


--- Page 49 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
46 of 120 
 
program to potentially interest the aggregator community, may make this 
1 
program a viable way to reduce, delay, or eliminate infrastructure 
2 
investment, especially when used in conjunction with other NPA 
3 
technologies.  In addition to supporting system pressure, gas demand 
4 
response has the potential to reduce the quantity of supply that may be 
5 
required on a peak day, which can provide cost savings.  In addition, 
6 
throughout the demonstration project, customers have been given access 
7 
to their usage data, providing them insights that they previously have not 
8 
had about their gas use.  Such insights may encourage greater 
9 
participation in the Companies’ energy efficiency programs and help to 
10 
achieve the REV objective of empowering customers to make more 
11 
informed energy choices. 
12

Q. 
What are the minimum values the Companies propose for customer 
14 
eligibility and reduction targets? 
15 
A. 
For KEDNY, eligible customers must consume at least 6,000 Dth per 
16 
year and commit to reducing demand over the duration of a peak event 
17 
by at least two Dth per hour (i.e., six Dth over the three-hour demand 
18 
response event).  For KEDLI, eligible customers must consume at least 
19 
4,000 Dth per year and commit to reducing demand over the duration of 
20 


--- Page 50 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
47 of 120 
 
an event by at least two Dth per hour (i.e., six Dth over the three-hour 
1 
demand response event). 
2 
 
 
3 
 
Customers will specify their committed reduction amount during the 
4 
application process.  The committed reduction must be greater than 10 
5 
percent of either the customer’s usage during a peak period or the sum of 
6 
the nameplate requirements of the customer, whichever is less.  This can 
7 
be determined based on either data from the Companies or data from 
8 
customer measurement systems.  Customers must achieve the minimum 
9 
reduction in each of the three hours of the event.  Reducing peak usage 
10 
more than the minimum may occur in any of the three hours. 
11

D. 
Sustainable Heat Initiative 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ Sustainable Heat Initiative. 
2 
A. 
The Companies propose a Sustainable Heat Initiative to ensure customers 
3 
have an opportunity to participate in the clean-energy future through 
4 
access to lower-carbon heating technology, while at the same time 
5 
supporting the efficient operation of the natural gas network.  The 
6 
initiative includes three components.  First, the Companies propose to 
7 
draw on the lessons learned from KEDLI’s current geothermal 
8 
demonstration project, by expanding the offering to interested customers, 
9 
giving priority to those located outside the footprint of the current and 
10 
planned gas networks.  This effort will help customers determine whether 
11 
geothermal technology may be a suitable NPA for meeting heating 
12 
requirements and facilitate customer access and adoption of geothermal 
13 
systems.   
14 
 
15 
Second, customers for whom geothermal is not the preferred option 
16 
would have the ability to participate in the Companies’ Clean Conversion 
17 
Program – a scaled-down version of the former Neighborhood Expansion 
18 
Program – intended to address anticipated customer demand for access to 
19 
gas heating, as opposed to higher emitting (and costlier) heating options 
20 
such as oil.  Third, for those customers facing economic barriers to 
21 


--- Page 53 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
50 of 120 
 
adopting lower-carbon heating technology, the Companies propose 
1 
expanding the Low-to-Moderate Income Gas Conversion Program (“LMI 
2 
Gas Conversion Program”) to provide incentives that will help customers 
3 
access cleaner, more efficient heating options. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
How will the Sustainable Heat Initiative benefit customers? 
6 
A. 
To meet the 80x50 goal and transition to a cleaner energy future, the 
7 
Companies believe aggressive action is required by both the Companies 
8 
and their customers.  In some cases, however, customers have few 
9 
options for meeting their heating requirements based on practical 
10 
challenges (e.g., proximity to clean energy sources) and economic 
11 
barriers (e.g., high upfront costs).  The Companies propose this initiative 
12 
as a comprehensive approach to meeting customer demand in a clean and 
13 
sustainable way.  It combines a robust NPA offering (i.e., geothermal) 
14 
with gas conversions and incentives, as a means of giving customers 
15 
additional clean-energy choices.  Taken together, the Companies believe 
16 
the initiative will achieve meaningful reductions in greenhouse gas 
17 
emissions. 
18 
 
19 
1. Expanded Geothermal Demonstration Project 
20 


--- Page 54 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
51 of 120 
 
Q. 
Please describe KEDLI’s current Geothermal Demonstration 
1 
Project. 
2 
A. 
The Geothermal Demonstration Project for KEDLI was adopted in the 
3 
2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases to test shared-loop ground-source 
4 
heat pump (“GSHP”) systems as a cost-effective heating and cooling 
5 
alternative to natural gas infrastructure in the Glenwood Village 
6 
community of Riverhead, New York.  The community is not currently 
7 
served by KEDLI’s gas network.  The geothermal technology used in the 
8 
project offers a way to reduce the carbon intensity of the heating and 
9 
cooling sector while providing homeowners with potentially significant 
10 
energy cost savings. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What results has KEDLI seen thus far from the project? 
13 
A. 
In the first year of the project, KEDLI connected a total of ten homes 
14 
with shared-loop GSHP systems, representing a total system heating 
15 
capacity of 30 tons.  The systems performed well during a period of 
16 
extended cold weather in January 2018, with customers not reporting any 
17 
loss in comfort.  Likewise, during prolonged periods of warm weather in 
18 
the summer, the systems did not encounter any interruptions or service 
19 
outages.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 55 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
52 of 120 
 
The systems used in the demonstration project have also achieved high 
1 
coefficients of performance (“COP”) in the range of 2.2 to 3.5.  High 
2 
COPs, such as those realized in the Glenwood Village community, are a 
3 
hallmark of GSHP, which leverages stable ground temperatures.  With 
4 
continued customer education and coordinated energy efficiency, the 
5 
project results suggest GSHPs have the potential to achieve COPs up to 
6 
4.   
7 
 
8 
Finally, customers identified comfort-related benefits, including 
9 
improved air quality, quiet equipment operation, simplicity, and more 
10 
even distribution of hot and cold air.    Further information from the first 
11 
year of the Geothermal Demonstration Project is included in Exhibit 
12 
___(FOH-4). 
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Why are the Companies seeking to expand the Geothermal 
15 
Demonstration Project? 
16 
A. 
Based on the project’s initial success, the Companies believe geothermal 
17 
technology has the potential to play an important role in the suite of 
18 
NPAs called upon to sustainably meet customer heating requirements.  
19 
The Companies estimate that approximately 200,000 residential 
20 
buildings are more than 200 feet from the Companies’ gas distribution 
21 


--- Page 56 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
53 of 120 
 
system and are therefore prospects for an alternative heating solution.  By 
1 
facilitating the installation of geothermal installations in areas of new 
2 
construction (i.e., areas where the existing gas distribution system is 
3 
unlikely to expand), as well as converting existing systems, the 
4 
Companies can provide further customer, system, and environmental 
5 
benefits. 
6 
 
7 
The Companies also believe that expanding the Geothermal 
8 
Demonstration Project, in partnership with geothermal installers and 
9 
other stakeholders, is key to animating the geothermal market, driving 
10 
further innovation, economies of scale, and reduced costs.  The reduction 
11 
of costs is critically important with the impending expiration of the 
12 
federal investment tax credit, which currently supports the installation of 
13 
GHSP systems.   
14 
 
15 
The Companies estimate each geothermal customer could realize an 
16 
average annual energy cost savings of between $1,000 and $1,500 and 
17 
nearly 6.75 metric tons of CO2 emission reductions from the 
18 
displacement of approximately 800 gallons of heating oil.  Finally, 
19 
geothermal also supports several REV objectives, including greenhouse 
20 
gas emission reductions in furtherance of the 80x50 goal, and aligns with 
21 


--- Page 57 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
54 of 120 
 
the Commission’s goal of reducing energy use by five TBtu through the 
1 
adoption of heat pumps. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
How do the Companies propose to expand the Geothermal 
4 
Demonstration Project? 
5 
A. 
The Companies propose to own and install the ground-loop portion of 
6 
GSHP system in parts of KEDNY and KEDLI’s service territories.  In 
7 
return, geothermal customers would pay the respective company a fee for 
8 
design work and company-owned equipment installed.  The expanded 
9 
project will prioritize LMI and C&I customers.  The Companies expect 
10 
the ground loop utility-ownership model will prove particularly useful, 
11 
helping customers to spread costs over time and improving adoption 
12 
rates.   
13 
 
14 
The goal of the expanded Geothermal Demonstration Project is to help 
15 
scale the market for this technology, reducing costs as economies of scale 
16 
are achieved.  The Companies would own the ground-loop portion of the 
17 
GSHP systems and would partner with qualified installers, similar to the 
18 
Companies’ Value-Plus Installers list, that could complete the above-
19 
ground/in-home work for customers.  In this way, the Companies would 
20 
assist in the development of the geothermal market.  Furthermore, the 
21 


--- Page 58 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
55 of 120 
 
Companies would seek to collaborate with industry stakeholders to 
1 
develop and adopt best practices for installing GSHP ground loops, with 
2 
specific consideration to how best to operate GSHP systems in an area 
3 
that has a high saturation rate of natural gas infrastructure.  The expanded 
4 
project is supported by a detailed data sheet included as Exhibit 
5 
___(FOH-1), Schedule 6. 
6

56 of 120

Q. 
Do the Companies propose to reconcile the revenue requirement 
6 
amount with the level of actual spending on the project? 
7 
A.

16

11 
and minimum customer connections requirements established in Case 14-

G-0214.

17

18 
additional steps toward meeting the 80x50 target while also displacing 
19 
significant quantities of heating oil.

15

16

KEDNY also offers an Excelsior Jobs Program, under which certified

--- Page 72 ---

2 
incremental gas usage for up to ten years.  Lastly, KEDNY offers an Area 
3 
Development Rate which provides distribution rate discounts of 35 
4 
percent for a five-year period to customers that locate or expand in 
5 
specific development areas of the KEDNY service territory. 
6 
 
7 
KEDLI offers a BIR under which eligible new and expanding customers 
8 
receive a distribution rate discount of 35 percent for a seven-year period, 
9 
after which time the discount is phased out over an additional three-year 
10 
period.  KEDLI also offers an Excelsior Jobs Program, under which 
11 
certified participants in the New York State Excelsior Jobs program 
12 
receive a marginal cost-based delivery rate (currently $0.184/therm) on 
13 
eligible incremental gas usage for up to ten years.

3 
V. 
Reducing Carbon Emissions on the Gas Network 
4 
Q. 
Please explain why the reduction of carbon emissions is an important 
5 
part of the Future of Heat strategy? 
6 
A. 
The Companies believe a holistic approach is necessary to drive 
7 
meaningful change toward a low-carbon future.  This approach includes 
8 
the aforementioned focus on network efficiency (e.g., reducing system 
9 
leaks) and demand-side energy management (e.g., energy efficiency), as 
10 
well as supply-side products capable of delivering carbon reductions on 
11 
the gas and electric networks.  As set forth below, the Companies intend 
12 
to accomplish this through the production and distribution of RNG, as 
13 
well as continued RD&D to drive further improvements in technology 
14 
and lower costs for customers. 
15 
 
16 
 
By including gas supply in their efforts to develop the low-carbon energy 
17 
system of the future, the Companies can provide customers access to 
18 
clean-energy choices that do not require deep retrofits or lifestyle 
19 
changes.  Moreover, adding the supply-side component allows the 
20 
Companies and local communities to beneficially use biogas created by 
21 


--- Page 77 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
74 of 120 
 
wastewater treatment and food waste, mitigating some of the 
1 
environmental impacts of these waste streams.  Finally, with the 
2 
development of P2G, the Companies can combine complementary 
3 
electric and gas technologies to enhance the environmental benefits for 
4 
both energy sectors through the production of lower-carbon RNG and by 
5 
using the gas network to store excess renewable energy. 
6 
 
7 
A. 
Renewable Natural Gas (“RNG”) 
8 
Q. 
What is RNG? 
9 
A. 
RNG is a term generally used to describe pipeline compatible gaseous 
10 
fuel derived from biomass or other renewable sources that has lower 
11 
lifecycle CO2e emissions than geological natural gas.  RNG feedstocks 
12 
include manure, food waste, wastewater treatment plants, or other 
13 
biomass sources, often using an anaerobic digester. With recent 
14 
advancements to lower the cost of gasification technology, feedstocks 
15 
with lower moisture content can also be used to produce RNG (e.g., 
16 
municipal solid waste or agricultural residues).  Furthermore, with new 
17 
technological innovations, production of RNG is moving beyond 
18 
biomass to include renewable electricity, often referred to as power-to-
19 
gas or P2G.  This concept includes either adding hydrogen to the existing 
20 
gas system (i.e., hydrogen blending) or producing synthetic methane by 
21 


--- Page 78 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
75 of 120 
 
combining hydrogen and carbon dioxide.  Collectively, RNG offers new 
1 
ways to decarbonize the gas network by reducing the carbon footprint of 
2 
the fuel supply in a manner similar to the way solar and wind technology 
3 
reduce the carbon footprint of electricity. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Please describe how the Companies have used RNG. 
6 
A. 
The Companies have over 30 years of experience integrating RNG into 
7 
the gas distribution network, starting with the Staten Island Landfill 
8 
project.  The Staten Island project – the oldest operating RNG facility in 
9 
the U.S. – continues to contribute RNG to National Grid’s distribution 
10 
network.  In addition, since 2009, the Companies have partnered with 
11 
NYC to deliver RNG from the City’s largest wastewater treatment plant.  
12 
In doing so, the Companies and NYC seek to animate the RNG market 
13 
and highlight opportunities for expanded use of this valuable energy 
14 
resource.  In 2010, National Grid published a white paper outlining the 
15 
potential for RNG to make a significant contribution to the reduction of 
16 
greenhouse gas emissions. The following year National Grid advised the 
17

1. 
Newtown Creek Project 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the Newtown Project and its current rate treatment. 
2 
A. 
The Newtown Project, located at the New York City Newtown Creek 
3 
Wastewater Treatment Plant, seeks to reduce greenhouse gas emissions 
4 
by promoting RNG as a long-term supply source.  KEDNY will capture 
5 
the biogas generated from the wastewater plant, which consists of 
6 
approximately 60 percent methane and 40 percent CO2, filter it through a 
7 
purification facility and upgrade it to pipeline quality natural gas.  The 
8 
RNG is then odorized and injected into the gas system for direct use by 
9 
customers.  
10 
 
11 
 
Because the in-service date and capital costs were not known at the time 
12 
rates were set in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, the costs for 
13 
the Newtown Creek project are not currently included in rates.  However, 
14 
the Joint Proposal authorizes KEDNY to defer the return of and on the 
15 
Newtown Project once it was placed in service.  In addition, the Joint 
16 
Proposal provides that the revenue requirement in a future rate case 
17 
would be subject to a potential $1.6 million annual exclusion (prorated 
18 
from the in-service date) for 20 years and reflect a 40-year depreciable 
19 
life.  However, the potential exclusion would be reviewed for possible 
20 
modification, with any property tax abatement or revenues from the sale 
21 


--- Page 81 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
78 of 120 
 
of gas or environmental credits produced by the Newtown Creek project 
1 
used as potential offsets to the exclusion.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What are the benefits associated with the Newtown Project, and how 
4 
do those benefits align with the State’s clean energy goals? 
5 
A. 
The Newtown Project will expand the concept of renewable energy to 
6 
more fully develop RNG as a viable alternative to conventional natural 
7 
gas resources.  In doing so, the RNG will enhance the diversity of the gas 
8 
supply, provide a new method for managing local waste resources, and 
9 
reduce greenhouse gas emissions in furtherance of the State’s clean 
10 
energy policy goals and the REV objectives.  In total, KEDNY 
11 
anticipates the Newtown Project will produce approximately 277,500 
12 
Dth of pipeline quality RNG, reducing CO2 emissions by approximately 
13 
16,000 tons annually.  In a separate partnership, NYC is working with 
14 
Waste Management to incorporate processed food waste into the 
15 
wastewater sludge at Newtown Creek.  The combined projects have the 
16 
potential to reduce annual CO2 emissions by 90,000 metric tons. 
17 
 
18

15 
Q. 
How does KEDNY propose to treat the project revenues? 
16 
A. 
As set forth in the Rate Design Panel testimony, KEDNY developed a 
17 
revenue mechanism to offset project costs through the sale of RNG 
18 
produced from the project, as well as any revenues realized from 
19 
monetizing the associated environmental attributes.  Specifically, Exhibit 
20 
___ (RDP-2) includes a revenue estimate of $1.9 million per year, 
21 


--- Page 84 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
81 of 120 
 
comprised of $1.0 million from the gas sales and $0.9 million from the 
1 
sale of environmental attributes. 
2 
 
3 
KEDNY proposes to true-up the actual revenues, such that any variances 
4 
will be deferred for future refund to (or recovery from) KEDNY 
5 
customers.  Any revenues realized above the level necessary to fully 
6 
reimburse customers for the project costs will be shared evenly between 
7 
customers and NYC beginning in the fifth year after the project becomes 
8 
operational.  Revenue sharing will then be assessed every year thereafter 
9 
for the remainder of the project.  KEDNY will track these costs on an 
10 
annual basis and compare to the project’s cumulative revenue requirement.   
11 
 
12 
Q. 
How did KEDNY calculate the estimated value of the environmental 
13 
attributes included in the Rate Design Panel’s revenue offset 
14 
mechanism? 
15 
A. 
KEDNY calculated the value of the environmental attributes by evaluating 
16 
the primary markets for RNG credits: (i) the federal RFS; and (ii) 
17 
California’s Low Carbon Fuel Standard (“LCFS”).  In 2019 the value of 
18 
RNG from food waste (classified as D5 under the RFS) is roughly $4/Dth, 
19 
and the value of RNG from wastewater (classified as D3) is roughly 
20 
$26/Dth.  These values, however, vary due to market changes, and 
21 


--- Page 85 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
82 of 120 
 
KEDNY expects significant variability in the market values over time.  
1 
The LCFS currently provides a financial incentive of approximately 
2 
$5.70/Dth, which can be additive to the RFS value if the RNG is 
3 
ultimately sold to a transportation customer in California.  Based on this 
4 
review, KEDNY conservatively estimates it will receive $0.900 million 
5 
per year in revenue from marketing the attributes.  The actual revenue 
6 
generated, however, is highly dependent on the volume of biogas 
7 
produced, what proportion of the volume is designated as D3 or D5, and 
8 
the price volatility of the RFS and LCFS markets.  
9

15 
the U.S. to directly inject RNG into a local distribution system, helping

17 
goals.  Between the property tax exemption KEDNY obtained from NYC 
18 
(discussed in the Shared Services Panel’s testimony) and the forecast 
19 
revenues set forth above, the project will deliver monetary and societal 
20 


--- Page 86 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
83 of 120 
 
benefits in excess of the $1.6 million exclusion.  Therefore, the 
1 
Companies believe the exclusion is not warranted.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
How has the Newtown Project supported RNG development? 
4 
A. 
KEDNY’s partnership with NYC on the Newtown Project was one of the 
5 
first of its kind in the U.S.  Although the partnership began with a focus 
6 
on helping NYC meet its environmental goals, it is also helping to 
7

8 
to incorporate RNG into the gas supply portfolio to achieve clean energy 
9 
goals, enhance reliability, and meet growing energy demands.  The 
10 
Companies’ have already started using lessons learned from the 
11 
development of the project to educate other utilities, RNG project 
12 
developers, and internal teams on how to plan, develop, and incorporate 
13 
RNG resources into the natural gas distribution system.

15 
 
 
2. 
P2G Demonstration Project (“P2G Project”) 
16 
Q. 
Please describe what is meant by the term “power-to-gas.” 
17 
A. 
P2G refers to the technical and economic potential of converting excess 
18 
renewable electricity to hydrogen or synthetic methane (i.e., RNG) and 
19 
utilizing the existing natural gas network to deliver the gas produced 
20 
using these renewable resources.  Indeed, P2G can serve as a form of 
21 


--- Page 87 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
84 of 120 
 
large-scale, long-duration energy storage when used to convert excess 
1 
renewable electricity that would otherwise be curtailed – a situation that 
2 
is expected to occur with increasing interconnection of renewable 
3 
resources including large-scale offshore wind – is converted into RNG.  
4 
P2G can also provide low- or zero-carbon RNG, depending on the 
5 
feedstocks used for production.  The technology holds considerable 
6 
promise for addressing clean-energy goals, as it has the potential to 
7 
support deep decarbonization of the transportation and heating sector, 
8 
which are two sectors of the economy that have proven challenging to 
9 
decarbonize. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please describe the P2G Project. 
12 
A. 
KEDLI proposes to develop a P2G design that combines existing 
13 
hydrogen production technology (i.e., an electrolyzer) and cutting-edge 
14 
methanation technology (i.e., a bioreactor) to produce pipeline-quality 
15 
RNG capable of meeting gas system requirements.  In doing so, KEDLI 
16 
seeks to partner with federal and local government (i.e., NREL and NYC, 
17 
respectively), as well as industry collaborators, such as Electochaea 
18 
GmbH, an innovative technology provider that develops bioreactors, to 
19 
design and engineer the P2G Project. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 88 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
85 of 120 
 
Q. 
What are the benefits associated with the proposed P2G Project, and 
1 
how do those benefits align with the Commission’s REV policy 
2 
objectives and the State’s clean energy goals? 
3 
A.  
Combining electolyzers and bioreactors to produce pipeline quality RNG 
4 
from renewable energy will help KEDLI lower carbon emissions, reduce 
5 
gas constraints, and provides a key outlet for excess renewable energy.  
6 
This last piece, the potential to store renewable energy, has taken on 
7 
growing importance with the accelerated offshore wind targets and on-
8 
shore wind and solar generation goals announced by Governor Cuomo in 
9 
the 2019 State-of-the-State address.  The last piece is key, as use of 
10 
renewable electricity to produce methane will prove an increasingly 
11 
valuable method of energy storage with the proliferation of distributed 
12 
generation and large-scale renewable energy projects.  In effect, the gas 
13 
distribution system will store excess renewable electricity that would 
14 
otherwise be curtailed.  Moreover, the RNG produced through the P2G 
15 
process will help offset sectors, such as heavy transportation and 
16 
industry, which have proven difficult to decarbonize. 
17 
 
18 
Q. 
What is the P2G Project schedule? 
19 
A. 
KEDLI will begin scoping, design, and engineering of the P2G Project in 
20 
2019, including the execution of agreements with project partners and 
21 


--- Page 89 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
86 of 120 
 
site owners.  KEDLI further anticipates full project design will be 
1 
completed in October 2023.  With design at 100 percent, KEDLI will 
2 
submit a proposal for recovery of the full project costs. 
3 
 
4

What is the projected cost of the P2G Project? 
5

6

7 
in the Rate Year, $1.825 million in Data Year 1, $0.725 million in Data 
8

9

10

11

12 
approval to move forward with project construction, deferring the 
13

14 
million proposed here, for recovery in KEDLI’s next rate case.  Any such 
15

16 
net plant tracker.

--- Page 90 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
87 of 120 
 
 
 
3. 
Hydrogen Blending 
1 
Q. 
What is hydrogen blending? 
2 
A. 
Hydrogen blending involves adding hydrogen into the gas system to 
3 
augment natural gas supplies.  The hydrogen is either produced from 
4 
water electrolysis using excess renewable electricity or from natural gas, 
5 
a process referred to as steam methane reforming.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Why do the Companies believe hydrogen blending is an important 
8 
part of the strategy for the low-carbon future of the heating sector?  
9 
A. 
Hydrogen blending holds promise for the future of the heating sector for 
10 
three reasons.  First, combusting hydrogen can have zero carbon 
11 
emissions if produced from renewable electricity.  Second, using 
12 
hydrogen blending as a low-carbon supply-side resource may prove to be 
13 
an efficient way to rapidly lower the carbon intensity of end-uses fueled 
14 
by natural gas without requiring the replacement of appliances or 
15 
performing extensive building retrofits.  Third, blending hydrogen with 
16 
natural gas, like P2G, could serve as a method for storing excess energy 
17 
generated by anticipated increases in renewable generation from solar 
18 
and wind; effectively reducing carbon emissions while bolstering gas 
19 
supplies. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 91 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
88 of 120 
 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ hydrogen blending project. 
1 
A. 
The Companies propose a hydrogen blending study in partnership with I-
2 
GIT.  The purpose of the study is to further analyze the impact of 
3 
blending hydrogen into the natural gas system, to determine the 
4 
percentage of hydrogen that can be safely blended into the Companies’ 
5 
gas distribution systems without negatively impacting the natural gas 
6 
network or customer appliances.  The Companies and I-GIT will evaluate 
7 
the technology and risks associated with hydrogen blending, establish 
8 
safety protocols, analyze the chemical changes to pipeline materials 
9 
created by different mixtures of hydrogen, as well as the operational and 
10 
engineering issues created by hydrogen blending.  The Companies 
11 
believe the hydrogen blending study is a necessary precursor to the 
12 
development of a hydrogen blending demonstration project. 
13 
 
14 
Q. 
What is the cost of the hydrogen blending study? 
15 
A. 
The Companies expect the hydrogen blending study to cost $0.446 
16 
million, split evenly between KEDNY and KEDLI to cover I-GIT’s 
17 
research work.  To offset a portion of that amount, the Companies and I-
18 
GIT submitted a concept paper to NYSERDA in response to Program 
19 
Opportunity Notice 3249.  NYSERDA accepted the concept paper, and 
20 
the Companies, along with I-GIT, submitted a full proposal for 
21 


--- Page 92 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
89 of 120

1

3 
Year, and would be split evenly between the Companies, as shown in 
4 
Exhibit ___ (FOH-13).

4. 
RNG Interconnections 
7 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ RNG interconnection proposal. 
8 
A. 
As set forth in KEDNY and KEDLI’s GIOP testimonies, the Companies 
9 
propose to leverage their technical expertise and economies of scale to 
10 
facilitate RNG interconnections by installing and owning parts of 
11 
distributed RNG facilities.  Like most renewable energy development, 
12 
RNG projects require large upfront capital investments, which can prove 
13 
an impediment to adoption.  A portion of the upfront capital needed for 
14 
RNG projects is due to the Companies’ equipment and engineering 
15 
requirements, which were established to ensure safe interconnection of 
16 
RNG projects.  To make it easier for customers to install low-carbon 
17 
RNG resources, the Companies propose engineering, installing, and 
18 
owning pieces of RNG interconnection equipment (e.g., meters, 
19 
odorizors, and spectrometers) at project sites.  
20 
 
21 


--- Page 93 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
90 of 120 
 
The Companies are also focused on shortening the interconnection 
1 
approval period to make RNG development a more efficient process for 
2 
project developers.  To that end, the Companies propose to create a new 
3 
Future of Heat Engineering department that will include additional FTEs 
4 
to review and process interconnection applications.  These additional 
5 
FTEs are discussed more fully below.  The Companies are currently in 
6 
discussions with six project developers looking to interconnect such 
7 
distributed RNG facilities to serve KEDNY and KEDLI customers with 
8 
feedstocks including municipal solid waste, food waste, and wastewater. 
9

B. 
Research, Development, and Demonstration (“RD&D”) 
1 
Q. 
Do the Companies’ participate in any end-use RD&D efforts? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies are active participants in two end-use RD&D 
3 
programs: (i) the Gas Technology Institute’s (“GTI”) Utilization 
4 
Technology Development (“UTD”) program; and (ii) I-GIT, which is a 
5 
unit of the Advanced Energy Research and Technology Center at Stony 
6 
Brook University.  
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies participation in the UTD program. 
9 
A. 
The UTD program is a utility-funded collaborative with 20 members 
10 
across North America.  It supports research, analysis, and deployment of 
11 
advanced 
technologies 
that 
are 
increasingly 
efficient 
and 
12 
environmentally beneficial, such as thermal heat pumps that cost-
13 
effectively reduce peak and greenhouse gas emissions.  Member utilities 
14 
may allocate their contributions to specific projects, such as thermal heat 
15 
pump research, that support their objectives.   
16 
 
17 
National Grid, for example, is the product champion for the Thermolift, 
18 
which is a client of the Clean Energy Business Incubator Program at 
19 
Stony Brook University.  The Thermolift research focuses on developing 
20 
a thermal heat pump capable of using RNG or solar to meet heating 
21 


--- Page 96 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
93 of 120 
 
needs.  Thus far, the Thermolift heat pump has exceeded its testing goals, 
1 
and drawn support from the NREL and NYSERDA.  Alternatively, UTD 
2 
participants may make a block contribution, known as the Sustaining 
3 
Members program, to a research portfolio determined by GTI.  
4 
Collectively, the UTD research is supporting long-term projects that 
5 
enable the use of RNG, hydrogen, and hydrogen blends.  The technology 
6 
investigated by the UTD collaborative includes items not eligible for 
7 
support from the Millennium Fund, or which are otherwise unsuitable for 
8 
funding by manufacturers. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Are there any costs associated with the Companies participation in 
11 
the UTD program? 
12 
A. 
Yes, as reflected in the Historic Test Year, the Companies each pay 
13 
$0.175 million in annual costs to participate in the UTD program.  The 
14 
Companies propose to continue their membership in the UTD program at 
15 
that level. 
16 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies participation in I-GIT. 
17 
A. 
The Companies, Stony Brook University, and other academic and 
18 
industry leaders launched I-GIT in 2018 as a comprehensive initiative 
19 
focused on finding clean, affordable energy solutions capable of meeting 
20 
growing clean energy demands.  I-GIT research goes beyond scientific 
21 


--- Page 97 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
94 of 120 
 
research, focusing on business and policy objectives related to the future 
1 
of the heating sector.  This work includes using I-GIT’s innovative 
2 
research, analysis, and education platform to accelerate deployment of 
3 
advanced energy technologies and infrastructure that will provide 
4 
community residents and businesses with value-added gas services.   
5 
 
6 
Q. 
What work has I-GIT accomplished in its first year of operation? 
7 
A. 
Since its launch in 2018, I-GIT supported development RNG projects 
8 
with dairy farmers, the utilization of RNG at State facilities, and the 
9 
integration of hydrogen into gas distribution systems.  In addition, I-GIT 
10 
reviewed the Companies gas REV demonstration projects.

1. System Efficiency  
10 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ proposed System Efficiency EAM. 
11 
A. 
The proposed System Efficiency EAM is composed of two metrics: (i) 
12 
Peak Reduction; and (ii) Supply Diversification.  The intent of the Peak 
13 
Reduction metric is to encourage gas demand reduction during peak 
14 
events, which will enable the Companies’ to focus commodity and 
15 
resources on constrained portions of the network. The Supply 
16 


--- Page 101 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
98 of 120 
 
Diversification metric will promote incremental development of local 
1 
pipeline quality gas (i.e., RNG). Increasing RNG interconnections 
2 
diversifies the Companies’ supply portfolio to include local sources, 
3 
resulting in a more resilient system.  
4 
 
5 
 
6 
 
7 
 
 
a. 
Peak Reduction 
8 
Q. 
Please explain the Peak Reduction metric. 
9 
A. 
The Companies’ proposed Peak Reduction metric will be calculated by 
10 
measuring reduction of gas usage during peak events and averaging the 
11 
reduction across all peak events called during the winter season as part of 
12 
the Companies’ expanded Demand Response Demonstration Project.   
13 
Reductions will be measured at participating customer sites, benchmarked against 
14 
customers’ weather-normalized baselines, and aggregated.   
15

6 
Q. 
How did the Companies calculate their Supply Diversification 
7 
targets?  
8 
A. 
Using attributes of the six RNG projects in KEDNY and KEDLI’s 
9 
interconnection queue, the Companies estimated the net benefit per Dth of 
10 
RNG connected to the natural gas distribution system.  Taking this 
11 
estimated benefit, the Companies then calculated the volume of Dths 
12 
needed to achieve one basis point of value each year.  The Companies 
13 
believe that connecting RNG results in overall societal benefits and 
14 


--- Page 105 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
102 of 120 
 
propose setting the minimum target at one basis point. To establish 
1 
midpoint and maximum targets, the Companies began by evaluating trends 
2 
in RNG interconnections from the 1980s to the present and projecting 
3 
future growth in RNG interconnections between 2020 and 2023 without 
4 
further action by the Companies.  The Companies then developed an 
5 
estimated trendline for accelerated RNG interconnections to establish the 
6 
midpoint and maximum targets.  Based on the foregoing, the Companies 
7 
propose a midpoint target of ten basis points, equivalent to a 300 percent 
8 
improvement in interconnection volumes.  The proposed maximum target 
9 
is capped at 20 basis points or a 500 percent improvement in 
10 
interconnection volumes.      
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What are the benefits associated with the metrics that comprise the 
13 
System Efficiency EAM?   
14 
A. 
The Peak Reduction and Supply Diversification metrics align with the 
15 
Commission’s finding that improving overall system efficiency is one of 
16 
the most important objectives of REV.  To that end, the initiatives and 
17 
strategies the Companies anticipate using to reduce peak and diversify 
18 
supply will likely result in avoided or deferred capital investment, active 
19 
customer participation in dynamic load management, reduced energy 
20 
constraints, and lower carbon emissions.  Moreover, the Supply 
21 


--- Page 106 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
103 of 120 
 
Diversification metric incentivizes the Companies to increase RNG 
1 
production volumes through market animation, creating a cleaner and 
2 
more diverse energy supply, providing a local source of renewable energy 
3 
that is consistent and reliable, and improving waste management.  
4

2. Energy Efficiency 
6 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ Energy Efficiency EAM. 
7 
A. 
The proposed Energy Efficiency EAM is composed of two metrics: (i) 
8 
Incremental 
Energy 
Efficiency; 
and 
(ii) 
Affordability. 
In 
the 
9 
Commission’s REV Track Two Order, the Commission suggested that 
10 
utilities consider applying the electric EAM approach to establish gas 
11 
efficiency targets, with comparable EAMs to be considered in gas rate 
12 
cases.  Consistent with the Commission’s direction, the Companies 
13 
propose a program-based Energy Efficiency metric measuring incremental 
14 
million British thermal units (“mmBtu”) reductions, as well as an 
15 
outcome-oriented Affordability metric measuring percentage reduction in 
16 
average low-income customer bills.   
17 
 
18 
 
 
a. 
Incremental Energy Efficiency 
19 
Q. 
Please describe how KEDNY and KEDLI will measure the 
20 
Incremental Energy Efficiency metric. 
21 


--- Page 107 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
104 of 120 
 
A. 
The Companies propose a program-based energy-efficiency metric 
1 
measuring incremental mmBtu reductions achieved through the programs 
2 
described in the Companies’ respective ETIP filings.  As the Commission 
3 
noted in the December 2018 EE Order, “[b]ecause increases in utility 
4 
targets require a lead time to allow vendors and service providers to ramp 
5 
up capacity, utilities will be granted flexibility in achievement of these 
6 
targets and expenditure of funds through the full 2019 - 2020 period.”  
7 
With the consideration that utilities should be granted flexibility to align 
8 
programs with increased targets, the Companies propose that the 
9 
Incremental Energy Efficiency metric begin in CY 2020 with the first year 
10 
EAM targets set to account for achievements in CYs 2019 and 2020 
11 
collectively.  Each year after will consider only achievements against the 
12 
EAM target in the respective year.

Affordability 
6

Please describe how the Companies will measure the Affordability 
7 
metric.

9

10 
customer bills.  This metric incentivizes the Companies for programmatic 
11 
efforts to reduce low-income customers’ energy usage and for animating 
12 
the market for third parties to likewise provide low-income benefits within 
13 
the Companies’ service territories. 
14

Q. 
Please explain how the Companies will calculate achievements 
16 
towards the Affordability metric.  
17 
A. 
The outcome-based Affordability metric measures reductions in the 
18 
average monthly customer bill for low-income customers.  The Companies 
19 
will calculate the average monthly customer bill on an annual basis and 
20 


--- Page 110 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
107 of 120 
 
compare the year-over-year performance to the weather-normalized 
1 
baseline from the previous year. 
2 
 
3 
Q.   
What do the Companies propose for their respective Affordability 
4 
targets and associated basis points? 
5 
A. 
The Companies propose that one basis point be awarded for each 0.2 
6 
percent reduction in the average low-income customer bill achieved year-
7 
over-year within their respective service territories.   
8

16

8 
3.  Carbon Reduction   
9 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ Carbon Reduction EAM. 
10 
A. 
The proposed Carbon Reduction EAM is composed of two metrics: (i) 
11 
metric tons of CO2e reduced from all full-service customers; and (ii) 
12 
metric tons of CO2e reduced from transportation customers.  This metric 
13 
will focus on the adoption rate of three specific offerings: oil-to-
14 
geothermal conversions; sales of RNG via the Green Gas Tariff; and use 
15 
of compressed natural gas (“CNG”) and RNG in NGVs to offset the use of 
16 
gasoline and diesel. 
17 
 
18 
a. 
CO2e Reduced From Full-Service Customers 
19 
Q.  
Please describe the full-service Customer CO2e Reduction metric.   
20 


--- Page 112 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
109 of 120 
 
A. 
Customers that are becoming KEDNY or KEDLI customers will typically 
1 
be switching their fuel source, usually from delivered fuels to natural gas. 
2 
This represents a reduction in CO2e for those customers.  The Companies 
3 
have an opportunity to influence those customers when they are making a 
4 
choice about their fuel switching so that their environmental impact will 
5 
be further reduced using RNG or geothermal systems. Additionally, the 
6 
Companies can market to existing customers to encourage them to explore 
7 
ways that they can reduce their environmental impact, either with RNG or 
8 
via replacing or supplementing their gas system with a geothermal system. 
9 
The combined impact of all these customers will be aggregated and will 
10 
provide a metric to measure how customers are tracking in terms of their 
11 
environmental impact. By encouraging customers to consider oil-to-
12 
geothermal or using RNG rather than traditional fossil-based natural gas, 
13 
KEDNY and KEDLI will be helping to reduce CO2e emissions. 
14 
 
15 
To calculate the carbon reduction, the volume of natural gas and RNG 
16 
sold to the appropriate rate classes will be calculated and summed at the 
17 
end of the calendar year.  This sum will be multiplied by the lifecycle 
18 
CO2e factor for the combustion of fossil-derived natural gas.  The resulting 
19 
number will be the baseline factor for the number of customers and the 
20 
amount of throughput that would have been expected in the absence of 
21 


--- Page 113 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
110 of 120 
 
environmentally beneficial offerings.  Next, the CO2e emissions for natural 
1 
gas and RNG will be calculated separately using the lifecycle CO2e 
2 
emissions factors for each feedstock. These two will be summed and the 
3 
resulting value will be subtracted from the first sum calculated above.  The 
4 
delta between these two will represent the carbon reduction achieved via 
5 
RNG. 
6 
To calculate the carbon reduction from geothermal systems, the number of 
7 
geothermal systems active and owned by each company will be calculated 
8 
based on the type of customer class they are serving.  The number of 
9 
systems will be multiplied by an average consumption of natural gas for 
10 
each of these customer classes.  The resulting theoretical consumption will 
11 
be multiplied by the lifecycle CO2e emissions factor for natural gas to 
12 
determine the amount of avoided carbon emissions via geothermal 
13 
systems. 
14 
 
15 
The two calculations will be added together to determine the total carbon 
16 
reduction from all full-service customers. 
17 
 
18 
Q. 
How did the Companies calculate basis points for the CO2e metric? 
19 
A. 
KEDNY determined basis point values by developing a 100-point scale 
20 
for achieving the carbon reduction target by 2023.  To the extent carbon 
21 


--- Page 114 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
111 of 120 
 
reductions are achieved at a level above the minimum target, the 
1 
Companies propose they earn 0.1 basis points for every 232 metric tons of 
2 
CO2e reduction. 
3

What does KEDNY propose for its full-service Customer CO2e

5 
A. 
KEDNY proposes the following targets:

KEDLI proposes the following targets: 
10

2020

11 
Q.  
Please describe the transportation CO2e Reduction metric.   
12 
A.   
The Companies currently serve CNG fueling stations, which supply fuel 
13 
for NGVs.  By switching from gasoline and diesel to CNG, fleets can 
14 


--- Page 115 ---
Testimony of the Future of Heat Panel 
 
 
 
112 of 120 
 
reduce their environmental impact.  However, this transition comes with a 
1 
cost in the form of higher vehicle costs.  With continued use of diesel as 
2 
the business-as-usual scenario, the Companies propose calculating the 
3 
reduction in CO2e by comparing CNG use to diesel.  The Companies 
4 
believe this calculation is reasonable regardless of whether customers are 
5 
full-service or work with an energy service company (“ESCO”), because 
6 
the Companies can determine whether customers are using natural gas for
